ParlaMint-DK_20150506130122	Thank you very much, and thank you very much for this opportunity to put this matter right. It is the case that total private employment in Denmark has increased by 39,000 people, to be precise, from the second quarter of 2013 to the fourth quarter of 2014. If a correction is made for the decrease in the average working time, the increase in employment of 31 000 people in the private sector, i.e. quite literally 31 000 people have been employed in the private sector with the same average working time as the other employees in the private sector. That is why it is not • and I would like to stress the fact that in the private sector it is not 40,000 mainly part-time jobs and study jobs, as the questioner claims. This is a real increase in employment. I think the questioner is apologized, as the questioner refers and generates his question on the basis of an article in Jyllands-Posten, and it does not edit the questioner, an article of 30 April 2015, which refers to a study by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Ministry of the Interior, which according to Jyllands-Posten was to be taken to account for the fact that the increasing employment in the private sector is primarily part-time and study jobs. However, this is a misleading interpretation of the study in question. It is the case that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Interior in their financial statement from August 2014 only examined the increase in employment from Q4 2012 to Q4 2013. However, the increase we have had since then of 40,000 people relates to another period, namely the period for the second quarter of 2013 to the fourth quarter of 2014. This does not, of course, take account of developments in 2014, and this is the very year in which we have seen a genuine recovery in the economy. Therefore, the investigation does not give a true picture of the full increase in employment of nearly 40,000 people that has taken place from the second quarter of 2013 to the fourth quarter of 2014. So when the questioner asks me to answer whether I am satisfied with 40 000 extra private jobs, the answer is yes. The answer is also that we need to move forward, and with the government's economic policy, we can look forward to an overall increase in private employment of 160,000 full-time people by 2020, and this can indeed happen, while giving priority to a space to further develop and develop the services we provide in the public sector.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506132105	Thank you for the question. At the same time, of course, I would also like to make it clear that the allocation of funds between the centres must be carried out in accordance with the applicable budgeting rules. Therefore, the FRIG pool for 2015 has been withdrawn in connection with the adoption of the Finance Act in December 2014, as the budget rules mean that no expenditure can be incurred without the legal basis of the Danish Parliament's adopted Finance Act for the financial year in question. In order to meet the financial security needs of voluntary centres as early as the beginning of the year, I announced the pool with a very short application deadline, and the processing of applications was initiated quickly thereafter with the expectation that the pool could be implemented in February 2015. We have discussed this with FriSe, who in turn is the member organisation of the voluntary centres. The case handling of the applications from the voluntary centres has unfortunately taken longer than expected and the pool was only implemented in mid-April 2015. The duration of the proceedings is due, inter alia, to the fact that many centres have not submitted in due time the material needed to process their applications. This concerns, among other things: on insufficient documentation of local co-financing, and of course we must have that in place. Therefore, the Ministry has asked the voluntary centres to provide the missing information, etc., so that the applications could be finalised and the voluntary centres could be notified of support. As I have said, this has helped to prolong the procedure, which is unfortunate in relation to the needs of the centres for budgetary security for the year. FriSe has been informed of the length of the proceedings and the reasons for this, and I therefore expect that next year's execution will take place more quickly and more smoothly.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506134831	Thank you, President. Does the Minister intend to make use of the right to object in Section 57 (3) of the Aviation Act? No. 2, No. 7, 1. In order to ensure better conditions for domestic passengers at Copenhagen Airport?
ParlaMint-DK_20150506134845	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506134846	It is true that I have the opportunity to give Copenhagen Airport a notice. I would like to lay down the framework for this specific provision. According to Paragraph 57, which is asked, permission to operate a public airport may be made subject to the special conditions deemed necessary. This is among other things. a condition that Copenhagen Airports A/S, which operates the airport, must ensure that the aerodrome meets Denmark's needs for national and international traffic connections at all times by offering the necessary capacity to settle air traffic. No. 2 7, p. 1 implies that, as Minister of Transport, in order to take account of essential social or general traffic considerations, I may, within a reasonable period of time, notify Copenhagen Airports A/S of a reasoned request to implement measures to ensure that the airport can meet the necessary capacity. The company may be required, with reasonable time, to provide an explanation of how it intends to implement the measure imposed. So there are three steps before you can give such a statement. The first step is to examine whether there are real challenges with regard to airport capacity. On the basis of the requests I have received, I have asked the Danish Road Traffic Authority to prepare a report on this, i.e. the consequences for the waiting time in the security check after the merger of the terminals. Next step: I have the opportunity to impose on the airport to implement measures that ensure capacity. Finally, the final step is that the airport may be required to provide an explanation of how they intend to implement a measure imposed. Such a person can, of course, be brought before the courts if the airport so wishes. It is clear that it is, of course, serious to take action of this kind. From the specific observations on the draft law on which the provision is based, it is expressly stated that both a requirement to implement measures and objections must be justified and that the authorities should therefore pay particular attention to the rules of the administrative court. It has therefore been stressed that there is a significant interference in the airport's right to self-determination. Because it is a private limited company where the state is a minority shareholder.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506135853	Thank you to the Minister and thank you to the author. We have now finished Question Time and can proceed to the first reading of motion for a resolution B 88.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506135900	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506135911	Thank you, President. Let me make one thing clear at the outset: I think that the issue of Danish culture and cultural rights in Denmark should be taken seriously, but what the Danish People's Party proposes to impose on the government is to make changes in laws, guides and rules to prevent discrimination against Danes and Danish culture in public institutions. The motion for a resolution is based on the premise that there is a need for legislation to prevent Danish citizens and Danish culture from being discriminated against, but that premise is fundamentally wrong. Danish legislation already provides citizens with protection against discrimination on the basis of, among other things: ethnicity, religion and gender. It is possible that the proposers do not have the same faith in Danish culture as I do, but Danish culture is strong, and Danish culture is firmly anchored in Danish society and in the Danish institutions and in the democratic processes associated with it. Finally, the motion for a resolution is based on the idea that public institutions and civil servants cannot manage Danish culture and Danish forms of interaction without the involvement of the Folketing, and I do not share that idea in any way. The comments on the motion for a resolution show that children must, as a matter of course, have liver pie, they must have meatballs and pork roast served when they are in kindergarten or crèche. And it goes on to say that decisions must not be taken locally to mark the festivals of other cultures, such as those in the United Kingdom. the Muslim eidfest. I would like to make it clear very briefly that I am not a minister of meatballs and for parties in Danish kindergartens, and I am never going to be a minister of meatballs. It is not for the Folketing to legislate on meatballs and Christmas tree parties. Quite frankly, there must also be limits to what we need to interfere in at central level. Here we are on the wrong side of the border. The task of the state is not to retail everything. However, I would like to stress that I think it would be strange if it were generally decided that pork should not be served in the Danish institutions. In the same way, I would also find it equally strange if it were decided that there were certain types of meat to be served. To be honest, it is best to find out locally. Then you can agree with or disagree with the decisions of local democracy, but I have a fundamental confidence that the institutions themselves can take into account both the majority and, as such, the few. In this context, I would like to stress that it is absolutely essential for me that we ensure that there is room for everyone in our society and that no one is discriminated against. I believe that together we must do our best to shape a society that creates communities that gather rather than separate. My task as Minister is precisely to strengthen the popular community and not to limit it. Then there is the question of the Danish forms of interaction. I have a basic confidence that public servants can find a way to greet other people respectfully when they act as their office. At the same time, I would like to stress that Denmark should not be a country where, because of misunderstood considerations, traditions and symbols are dropped so as not to offend new fellow citizens. We must stick to our traditions and we must stick to our values. That is why, as a matter of fact, I also called back in 2013 for the matter of the censor, which would not shake hands with women students, to be brought before the Equal Treatment Board. And I am also very pleased that the Equal Treatment Board found that the refusal to shake hands with women students was covered by the equal treatment law prohibiting harassment on grounds of sex. The decision remains extremely important and sends a very strong signal that we in Denmark have gender equality and that public employees must not discriminate against women or men. To sum up, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. Danish culture and Danish associations live in the best way without detailed management here from the Danish Parliament. The Danish culture is based on the values that apply in this country, and a key value is that we do not accept unbiased discrimination, regardless of who is behind it and whoever is affected. Thank you very much for the floor.

In the same way, I would call on local politicians from all parties who sit on the city council and help to guide and benefit the institutions, to engage in management and to demand the way in which they think the institution should function. Against this background, I would like to say that we believe that Danish culture is important. We do not believe that Danish culture can be encapsulated into one specific group of foods or one specific mode of action. Culture is evolving all the time. We have some traditions and some guiding values that we must safeguard and safeguard, but one of our strongest values is democracy, commitment, popular opinion, and, on that basis, I do not believe that the right way to protect Danish culture is to implement the Danish People's Party's motion for a resolution. ParlaMint-DK_20150506143124	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it is the Enhedslisten's rapporteur, Lars Dohn.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506143134	Thank you. I think it's the temp parade today. I shall read the Enhedslisten's rapporteur, Johanne Schmidt-Nielsens, speak out, and it starts like this: Enhedslisten cannot support the Danish People's Party's motion for a resolution. The proposal revolves around three issues: food served in public institutions, such as: crèches and kindergartens, the way in which public servants greet citizens, and the celebration of religious festivals in schools and day care institutions. Whether all three conditions apply, Enhedslisten does not want them regulated or controlled centrally, more than is already the case. As far as food in day care facilities is concerned, it is of course important that children are offered healthy and tasty food and that this is done in respect of parents' religious beliefs, including if they are Jews or Muslims and do not eat pork because of their religion. In the view of Enhedslisten, how to solve this question must be a whole local matter. Typically, it is the head of the institution and the parents' board who together find a solution so that all children and parents feel welcome in the institution. In some cases it may be up to the city council to decide on the diet policy of the day institutions, but it should not be a matter for the Folketing other than the general requirements I have described here. The question of how public servants greet citizens became topical in the summer of 2013, when a censor at Herning HF and VUC announced before the exam in mathematics that he would neither shake hands with women nor men when his faith forbade him to shake hands with women. One of the female examiners complained to the Equal Treatment Board, she was successful and was awarded an allowance; when the censor refused to shake hands, it was harassment, the mention was made. The case has therefore been settled within the framework of the applicable law. In the comments to the proposal, the authors of the proposals make much of the Herning censor case. It would have been fair to mention that the Equal Treatment Board has established under the law in force that it was unacceptable that the censor did not want to shake hands with women. In the view of the Enhedslisten, there is no need for further legislation on the manner of greeting of employees. The third element is the celebration of religious festivals in schools and in day care institutions. In the Enhedslisten, we do not want central regulation in this area. Generally speaking, we believe that integration must go both ways, considering that it is natural for children in a day-care institution or at a school to learn about each other's festivals. The main point, however, must also be that we in the Folketing should not determine whether there may or should be Advent crane in a yellow living room, or whether Jewish or Muslim festivals should also be celebrated. The decision is best made by educators and parents' boards. The Enhedslisten will vote against the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506145254	The debate is open. The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506145302	Today we are dealing with a motion for a resolution tabled by the Left and the Conservatives. With this proposal, the two parties are calling, among other things, for: the government to remove the livelihoods of the cooperatives, to put it plainly: the maximum price. It is intended to enable shareholders to take the price of a cohabitation which they would be able to obtain from a free sale. If I may put it that way, the cooperatives will be treated equally with the owner-occupied housing when it comes to the price that must be taken for them. It's gonna pull our cities off socially. One cannot understand in the Left and Conservatives that the shareholders cannot be allowed to take the price of their shares that the buyers are willing to pay. The proposal argues here that the maximum price means either that shareholders are left with unsellable shares, if the maximum price is higher than the market price, or that money is traded under the table and secret waiting lists if the maximum price is lower than the market price. To put it bluntly, it is nonsense in my optics and it clearly shows that the Left and Conservatives have not understood what the maximum price is for. I would, of course, like to use my speaking time to explain this here in the Folketing. First of all, I would like to make it clear that the government's efforts in recent years to restore security and confidence in the cooperative housing market have given rise to so much. There are again good winds in the housing market, and there are continuous reports that the lay times for the co-operative homes are decreasing and that they are increasingly being sold without refusal. So we have taken care of the uncertainty created during the crisis of the '00s, which was on the market, and have secured a stable market with new rules on key information and stricter requirements for the creation of cooperative housing associations. The price which each shareholder may charge for his or her share covers his or her share in the total assets of the association. In addition, the improvements made by the shareholders in the apartment can be added. And that is the price that the shareholder in question may charge to the cooperative. The maximum price does not mean that the shareholders are bound to take that particular price. It is only a rule that ensures that the price cannot be higher than the share in the fund's assets. And why is that? Well, the most important thing is to ensure the maximum price that we have a large intermediate group of homes, especially in our large cities • Yes, in Copenhagen it is up to 33-34 per cent. of the total housing stock, which is cohabitation, i.e. a great many cohabitation units, in which people with ordinary incomes can live and have the opportunity to live. A substantial abolition of the maximum price would lead to substantial increases in the price of cooperative housing. The average price increases at national level are between 50% and 70%. Yes, we've run this model through the DREAM model, that is, the model used to determine the average rent, and we've also been looking at the differences in the maximum price and the differences in the market value they're considered to represent. Here, according to one model, it will rise to up to 66 per cent. at national level, 75%. in Copenhagen and up to 90%. in certain areas of Copenhagen. Of course, there will also be regional differences between the average price increases in Copenhagen and in Frederiksberg, but our co-operative housing will increase between 70 and 90%. So, although there are of course always a number of different conditions and there are also uncertainties, it has to be said that the abolition will lead to so much price increases that the cohabitation will no longer really be the economically manageable form of housing between rental and owner-occupied dwellings, as the Left and Conservatives claim, and which we all hope to continue to do; yes, that is something that can be called into question. It is therefore certain that the removal of the maximum price will allow the present shareholders to gain a capital gain in a sale. However, that is not good enough reason for me, the disadvantages are far too great. It will be harder, it will be more expensive for new co-buyers to enter the housing market and where the profits will only come once, namely for those who are standing and selling right now, we will be left with a much more expensive cooperative housing market. It will only be interesting for those people who have so much money that they might as well buy a owner's residence. This will also have major consequences for the rest of the housing market. The abolition of the maximum price will make it more attractive for tenants to establish a cooperative housing association. This would mean a reduction in rental properties with private rentals and a corresponding increase in the number of cohabitants. It could also be seen as a desire to dismantle the private rental sector, and there will hardly be any doubt that the landlord side here will not look passively if it becomes more attractive for the tenants to buy and convert a property into a cooperative housing association, just as they will of course not remain passive if they are directly pressured on the return they have on their rental property. In both cases, it is expected that the landlords will press to increase the return on the property's primary operations, i.e.: press for higher rents and possibly an entire rent fixing. We know that, among other things. The Conservatives want it. We will also see a cohabitation that is more similar to the condo. There will be an increase in the supply of housing, which is traded under market conditions, but under different conditions. The natural consequence of the economic equality of cohabitants with owner-occupied dwellings must also be that the authors of the proposal did not think of a fiscal equality in relation to the payment of property tax. I do not think there are many shareholders who will be interested in that equality. It cannot be ruled out that the marketization, as we call it, of cohabitants will mean a fall in the prices of condos, especially in areas with a very large number of cohabitants. Moreover, one might ask the authors of the motion for a resolution whether this is really not a form of circumventing the ban that we have against dividing the elderly housing stock into condominiums, because then, suddenly, the units of ownership are actually divided into condominiums. At any rate, I would like to say that I think it smells to me that the Left and Conservatives want total liberalisation of the housing market. I guess you might well ask who's gonna live in our big cities. Conservatives and the Left voted against our being able to use the law of plans as a tool for building more general housing, using a special tool to build new general housing in places where the land is too expensive, and now we want to turn the cooperative housing into owner's apartments. Are they the only ones who can afford to live in larger cities? I think, of course, that this is an honest matter, so I just think that the Left and Conservatives should stand by it instead of pretending, as in their proposals, that they want to do something really good for the cooperatives. Because the effect of this will be that we put the cohabitation form in the grave, and presumably after a short time the cohabitation units will disappear completely. I would like to make it as clear as I can that it is my view that we must maintain a varied housing market, where co-operative housing is a real and affordable alternative to owner-occupied housing. Let me therefore make it as clear as I can: I and the government will not be helping to remove the maximum price of cooperative housing. Then there is also a question from the Left and the Conservatives that they will allow mortgage holders to take over a number of cooperative housing for foreclosures on the grounds that, with this greater certainty, banks would like to lower interest rates for cohabitants. Again, I would like to say that this seems like it's gotten out of thin air, I don't know where you get this thesis from, but we have a 2010 report in which it is concluded that it can't be clearly demonstrated that the uncertainty about the compulsory auction conditions of the mortgages affects their willingness to grant loans for the purchase of cooperative housing. And it is a 2010 report made under the proposers' own government that says that it cannot be demonstrated here. Moreover, few financial institutions consider that it is important to fix interest rates in relation to the loan part whether or not a cooperative housing association has a residence requirement in the association's statutes. And most importantly, it is already possible for the individual cooperative housing association to include a provision in the statutes of the association which allows the landlords to overearn a share in a foreclosure. I have also been informed that a great many associations have such regulations. Finally, with their proposals, the Left and the Conservatives want to make the Danish Business Authority's accounting guide for cooperative housing associations a mandatory standard for the cooperative housing associations. According to the proposers, it should facilitate the work of associations and make the purchase and sale of cooperative housing more manageable. I do not think that is the case again. The government has not wanted to introduce an obligation for associations to use an external auditor, and it has to be seen in the light of the fact that it has been pointed out that this can be really burdensome for many of our small cooperative associations. According to the Accounting Guide, it can be used freely by all cooperative associations, whether or not there is an external or internal auditor attached to it. According to the information I have, the accounting guidance has already been widely used among accountants, cooperative housing associations and management companies. It is probably relatively few associations that today do not have an external auditor attached, but for these smaller associations it must be assumed that the incentive to use the accounting guidance at least will be large, thus ensuring access to some of the key information that was introduced in 2013 to be provided in the context of a cooperative housing transaction. Moreover, I have every confidence that the new rules on disclosure and the stricter rules on the formation of cooperative housing associations which we have introduced and which entered into force on 1 January of this year will create greater transparency and thus contribute to an economically sound cooperative housing market. Against this background, I do not believe that there will be any need to introduce a general obligation to use the accounting guide, which will be burdensome especially for our small cooperative associations. I will, of course, follow it up if there is a need, but I do not think there is a need now. So the central part of the proposal that we are discussing today will, in my opinion and in the opinion of the government, result in the prices of cohabitation units exploding. The cooperative housing sector will be buried and eventually disappear, and I want to make it clear: the government does not want that. We regard the other elements of the proposal as unnecessary in relation to the regulation we already have in the area of cohabitation today. I do not think it comes as a surprise that I say: the government certainly cannot vote in favour of this proposal.

In relation to the proposal to make the Danish Business Administration's accounting guidance compulsory, I would say that this is also a balancing exercise. Firstly, the accounting guidance has already been widely used among accountants and cooperative associations and management companies, and there are probably relatively few associations which today do not have an external auditor attached to it. Secondly, not long ago, the government has introduced new rules on disclosure obligations and stricter requirements for the formation of cooperative housing associations, which came into force here on 1 January of this year, with the aim of creating greater transparency and thus helping to ensure an economically sound cooperative housing market. We would like to keep that in mind and we would like to discuss whether there is more that can or must be done, but we have little difficulty in seeing that this proposal should now be the solution. I would therefore like to welcome the radical rapporteur and say that the Radical Left would like to take part in a debate on the future of the cooperative housing market. We would also like to take part in discussing whether we can deregulate the cooperative housing market and the housing market in its entirety, but the proposal from the Left and the Conservatives, in fact, sees most of all as a gift to those who happen to own or even have a share today, without looking at the advantages and disadvantages and effects for the future of the cooperative housing market and the housing market as a whole, which is why we cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506164018	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it is the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Louise Schack Elholm, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506164034	Thank you for all the comments. The debate that we had was not so surprising. The points of view are, after all, relatively well known. It is, of course, a pity that the Minister chooses to conduct a study which the Minister does not even publish, so that the Folketing knows what we are discussing. It would suit the Minister that he published them before he started launching studies with various figures, so that we actually had an opportunity to debate them on a factual basis. But no, you have to find that elsewhere. I thank you for the positive comments that have been made on parts of the proposal. I thank them very much, and I will take them into further work. In general, I would like to thank you for the interesting debate. I very often receive enquiries from citizens who are dissatisfied with the cooperative housing market. They think that the market is opaque and they think that cooperative housing loans are too expensive. These are problems that we need to resolve, and I am pleased that we finally have the area on the agenda today. First of all, I would like to make it clear that the Left considers the cooperative residence to be an important part of a broad and varied supply of housing. The cooperative residence, for example, is: suitable for those who weigh community high. It may be older people who need association and joint activities with other citizens, or it may be younger people who want to have more codecision than they do in a rental home. That is why it is important for the Left that the cohabitation be maintained in roughly the same condition as we see today. So today's resolution is not an attempt to abolish the cohabitation. On the contrary, we see it as a helping hand for an area that needs a constructive overhaul because we are experiencing a great deal of opaqueness and a problem of money under the table. The motion for a resolution is also to be seen in the light of the Left's cooperative housing proposal of September last year. Four proposals were presented to ensure more transparency and cheaper loans in the cooperative housing area. Three of these proposals are contained in this motion for a resolution. In short, the proposals are to allow cooperatives to sell at market prices, to make the Danish Business Authority's guidance on annual accounts a mandatory standard, and to give banks the right to purchase the cooperative housing for which they have granted loans at foreclosure. The first proposal, after today's debate, is to judge the most controversial. The proposal is that cooperatives, if they so wish, should have the opportunity to sell at market prices. Today, it is often difficult to find out the price of a cohabitation. In the cooperative housing sector, three different ways of fixing the maximum price are being worked out. The maximum price is the official price at which to sell a cohabitation. There are three methods: you can start from the price after purchase price, i.e. what you paid for the property when you bought it; it is typically the lowest price that you can set as the maximum price. You can choose to follow the public rating • it is the public rating, which has been criticized enormously in the media, from 2012 and which has been judged to be quite biased or you can choose a value assessment based on the property's value as a rental home. To this must be added the improvements made by the cohabitant in the apartment, but they are not included in the technical maximum price, but in the maximum price received in the situation in question. The problem with the maximum price is that the cooperatives are not priced according to their real value, and this has some unfortunate consequences. First, the shareholders risk having a fictitious valuation that does not reflect the real value of the dwelling. Location has e.g. It is not an immediate matter for the value of the dwelling, just as in many places public assessments are used, which no one really believes to be fair. Secondly, there is the risk that cooperative housing associations will be left with unsellable shares and high housing charges. In fact, when the maximum price is set too high, there will be a tendency to believe that the real value of the cohabitation is the maximum price, although this is not really the case. And in such cases, you will have too much debt and unsellable shares. Thirdly, there is the risk that the maximum price will be set too low. In such cases, cooperative housing will be traded through secret waiting lists and with money under the table. Today, in fact, it is often the case that if you want one of the most attractive cooperatives, you need to know someone who knows you and so on, and even in that case you risk having to pay $50,000. for a carpet from Ikea like some kind of money under the table. That is why the motion for a resolution proposes that cooperative housing associations should be given the opportunity to have cooperative housing prices follow the market price. This will lead to greater transparency. At the same time, problems with unrealistic official prices and money will be avoided under the table. The government is clearly not in favour of having cooperatives sell at market prices. That is why it can be said today that the abolition of maximum prices will increase the prices of cohabitation units by 66 per cent. at national level and 75% at national level. in Copenhagen. According to the government, this will make it more difficult for low- and middle-income recipients to access the cooperative housing market, especially in Copenhagen. The government's calculations are extremely questionable. It is assumed that the prices of cooperative housing will rise to the level of the prices of owner-occupied dwellings. However, they will not. We have clearly been made clear today that the Socialists are also not aware of the difference between owner-occupied housing and cooperative housing. But let me be clear. There are plenty of conditions that mean that cohabitation units will have a lower value than owner homes. You buy, for example. not a dwelling, but a right to use a dwelling, and as a shareholder one is liable for the whole of the association's economy. This puts you at greater risk. You have common costs in the cooperative housing association, and you have more expensive loans and more risk than you have with owner-occupied housing, and you often have no or little opportunity to rent the accommodation if you are to leave temporarily. In other words, the government's figures are purely scaremongering. Let's get some facts on the table instead. In connection with the publication of the Vedest's cooperative housing document, Realkredit Denmark stated that the purchase price of every second bid in the second half of 2013 was the same as the maximum price. So not all cooperative homes are sold at the maximum price. Therefore, not all cooperative housing associations can expect higher prices by removing the maximum price. Realkredit Denmark also calculated how much the prices of cohabitants will increase if it is assumed that the total monthly expenditure will rise to the same level as for owner-occupied dwellings. So it is not exactly our proposal that they have calculated, but something about what the Minister is proposing. The calculations showed that the cooperative housing in the Capital Region will increase by 12%. These are, after all, somewhat different figures from those which the Minister has come up with. These figures are supported by the Housing Economic Knowledge Centre. They have previously estimated that prices in the cooperative housing market in Copenhagen will increase by around 10% if prices are set free. Let me stress here that these are only official sales prices, not real sales prices. There is a widespread black economy in this area, and it is not sufficiently elucidated. In addition, it can be said that most recently here today the housing economist in Nordea Lise Nytoft Bergmann has also refuted the government's claims. She does not believe that abolishing the maximum price would destroy ordinary people's dream of a residence in Copenhagen. She rightly points out that there are many cohabitation units that are negotiated via private networks that are rarely accessible to low- or middle-income recipients. At the same time, she predicts that there will actually be more housing opportunities for those seeking housing. In addition, it can be said that this proposal also entails cheaper bank loans, and this will greatly benefit the low and middle-income groups. Government estimates of 66 per cent. at national level and 75% at national level. in Copenhagen is probably shot far, far above the target. The same is true of the claim that low- and middle-income recipients will be disadvantaged. It's just a scaremongering campaign. The government hasn't even published their calculations openly. It also indicates that this is political commissioning rather than real analysis. Of course, the left is not interested in increases of up to 75%. in the cooperative housing market, which we also find totally unrealistic. The intention of this proposal is to ensure transparency and openness in the cooperative housing market. That is why we are prepared for thorough committee work so that we can hear experts about their assessment of the cost of housing and the amount of money under the table. It is essential for the Left that ordinary people are not excluded from the cooperative housing market. That is precisely what this proposal should do, but we are prepared to look into it. The second proposal is that the Erhvervsstyrelsen's guidance on the annual accounts of cooperative housing associations should be made compulsory. Today it is necessary for cooperative housing associations to provide financial statements. The annual accounts must meet a number of general requirements of the Financial Statements Act, but there is no requirement for the preparation of the financial statements themselves. This means that the annual accounts of cooperative housing associations are very different, and this makes it difficult for buyers to see through the accounts of the various associations. The motion for a resolution therefore proposes that the Danish Business Authority's guidance on the annual accounts of cooperative housing associations be made compulsory. The Danish Business Authority's guidance on financial statements contains a model account which cooperative housing associations will then use as a template for their own financial statements in the future. This will facilitate the work of cooperative housing associations and will make the purchase and sale of cooperative housing more transparent. The third proposal is that banks should have the right to buy the cooperative housing for which they have granted loans at foreclosure. This right is guaranteed by removing the residence obligation of cooperative housing associations in the event of foreclosure. Today, shareholders can only take bank loans and not mortgage loans as ordinary home owners. This is due to the fact that each cohabitant cannot mortgage its cohabitation because it is owned by the cooperative housing association, and therefore the shareholders have to settle for the expensive bank loans. Most often, the more percentage points are more expensive, and this naturally creates great dissatisfaction among the shareholders. With this proposal, we expect to be able to reduce the cost of bank loans and thus respond to some of the problems experienced by cohabitants with lending.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506165923	The debate is open. It is first the Minister for Town, Housing and Rural Affairs. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506165944	Here we go again. After all, this is a partial re-submission of B 15, which we discussed in 2012 and voted on in 2013. At that time, the government voted against the proposal, and it ended up being rejected. I would like to say that we could start a little at the top, and I would like to do that in order to put this debate a little more into perspective. There is no doubt that, during the crisis, many jobs have disappeared in the primary industries of our rural areas, which is of particular concern to our inhabitants in rural and peripheral areas. The crisis was probably linked to urbanisation, the shift from country to city, and the crisis that hit our outermost regions is something that we in the government have taken very seriously. That is why, as you know, we have also taken a large number of initiatives to improve the framework conditions in rural areas. We have among other things. with various growth packages, on which we have agreed with several parties of the Folketing, strengthened the private labour market and created growth plans, and therefore it is important to know, when discussing these things, that it is the private jobs that really matter in rural and peripheral areas. As I explained in connection with B 15 at the time, we are not opposed to the creation of state jobs in rural areas. State jobs can help stimulate local and regional development in a given area. In my own ministerial area, I also established a secretariat in Nykøbing Falster for the local action groups earlier this year. So far it has provided 16 state jobs in Nykøbing Falster, and the LAK Secretariat was set up as part of a reorganisation, bringing together all those who had to do with these local action groups. It made very good sense to look at where the jobs could be placed most appropriately. Specifically, it made great sense for me to place the LAK Secretariat outside one of the major cities. So we in the government believe that the location of state jobs should be considered in particular in the context of reorganisations or in the context of the same tasks in the state in order to ensure a more effective performance of tasks. It is on the basis of the reorganisations and restructurings that I think it should be assessed in particular. When you do that, you have to do it from among other things. growth, development and effective performance. The economy must make good sense, because it is also important to place state jobs in the outermost regions, if that makes sense. However, this does not mean that at all costs state jobs must be transferred to rural areas or that new jobs should be created automatically elsewhere. Today is more than half, approx. 56 per cent. of the state jobs, located outside the Capital Region. It is our view that the question of further relocation of state jobs must be based on an in-depth analysis and an assessment of the professional and economic situation in the specific cases, including the possibility of ensuring synergy with local and regional industry in the areas to which jobs are relocated. I will then briefly describe some examples of what might be relevant in relation to the appropriate location of government jobs. The examples below are also set out in the 2014 Regional and Rural Policy Statement, which we will be discussing shortly. Firstly, an institution's tasks and stakeholders can make a particular location appropriate. For example, there may be a need for state institutions providing ministerial and parliamentary services to be located at Slotsholmen. On the other hand, there may also be geographical considerations which make the location outside the metropolitan area appropriate. In other situations, the location has no bearing on the solution of the institution's tasks and an institution could be located in all regions of the country where there is sufficient skilled labour. Secondly, there may be an important consideration to take in terms of location opportunities when looking at achieving a higher efficiency or quality in the institution's task solution. For example: The merger and relocation of administrative functions can lead to economic gains in terms of lower rent, higher productivity and a more professional professional environment. Therefore, any relocation may be relevant to consider in the context of reorganisation of administrative tasks. Thirdly, there is one consideration that I think should be taken into account when considering the relocation of state jobs, namely that the economic costs must be proportionate to the economic gains. Faced with the cost of the relocation, of course, is the possibility of lower rent, more efficient and better assignments, along with the possibilities for developing growth in the recipient area. However, it is important for the government that the economy in the context of the relocation process as a whole is such that it is done on a responsible basis. Fourthly, of course, there is the need to be able to retain the specialist knowledge and the right competences in relation to the institution's tasks. There is no doubt that there may be a risk of loss of competence in relation to the relocation of state jobs. If it is not possible to attract the necessary specialist skills, it can, in the extreme, have a negative impact on the institution and the quality of the task carried out. Fifthly, it is relevant and relevant to take into account the need to have a real match in the local area, so as to ensure that there is increased demand for products and services and increased employment in the neighbourhood and synergy with local actors. That is why it is basically when you look at the relocation of state jobs, to Esbjerg, Odense, Aalborg and the larger and medium-sized cities. It's very rare that what I've done happens. I put it in Nykøbing Falster, and you do this. Perhaps it is a saga simply to leave them outside a place where there is a professional environment. That is all we need to have in the discussion, that is, that it is not completely out in the individual rural areas. It is typical in a larger city where you can spar with the professional environment and where you can attract skills that are proper in relation to this part of it. So there may be a large number of other important issues which are relevant to include in the specific cases which I have not mentioned here either. However, there may be a special purpose for relocation and there may be other considerations which are considered essential in the specific case. However, it is important here that, overall, when moving out jobs, there is a solid and proper basis for a decision to move out a specific state institution, also in order to prevent any gains from relocation being lost. Finally, I would like to draw the House's attention to the fact that a decision has been taken in connection with the examination of motion for a resolution B 17 on a plan of action for the strengthening of the outermost regions, in which, among other things, we are carrying out an analysis. discusses the issue of the relocation of state jobs. It is part of this analysis. That is why I do not think it is particularly relevant this time to carry out a separate analysis, because there we have a broader analysis in which state jobs are included in the analysis we have carried out and adopted together with a majority in the Folketing.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506175712	Thank you. Since the radical rural rapporteur, Helle Løvgreen Mølvig, could not be here, I have promised to read her speech. The motion for a resolution here is aimed at requiring the government to set up a working group to draw up a plan for the relocation of state jobs to the neighbouring and rural municipalities of the country. In addition, the proposal calls for the working group to propose which jobs and educational establishments can be placed in the province with advantage. A similar proposal has been made earlier in the 2012-13 Assembly and then it was voted down. In the Radical Left, we take rural areas seriously and we strive to ensure good conditions for business so that companies in Denmark dare to invest in jobs locally. We have had several debates in the Folketing on the problems that exist in the peripheral and rural municipalities, and we recognise that we can make a difference with the policy we are pursuing. There is already a very long list of initiatives aimed at creating better conditions for rural areas implemented by the government. The measures are wide-ranging and have had an effect on a wide range of areas. However, when the Radical Left wants more initiatives to be taken to address the problems arising in the wake of rural demographic developments, we think that this should be done on an enlightened basis and in an overall strategy. The government is currently working on a comprehensive analysis, which we have already discussed several times among the rapporteurs, who among other things. We also need to look at the possibility of relocating state jobs and to come up with a list of possible initiatives. The working group will make its recommendations in September, and for this reason we see no need to set up another working group to look separately at the relocation of state jobs. Regional and rural policy statement 2015, which we will be discussing here shortly, states in the section on state jobs that the placement of state jobs must be carried out under balanced considerations. In addition to the concern for the employment and economy of specific areas, for example, consideration is given to the availability of skilled labour, access to day-to-day collaborators and economic considerations. Finally, an underlying consideration is to achieve the maximum efficiency in solving public tasks. In the Radical Left, we are not opposed to the relocation of state jobs. On the contrary, we have helped to make decisions on the relocation of more jobs. However, we would also like to say that we attach importance to the fact that this does not just happen blindly. We would like to take this into account when it comes to the creation of new institutions or future reorganisations, or when tasks are put together across the state, but this must be done on the basis of an overall strategy where it makes sense humane, economic and practical. That is why we cannot support this proposal.

It makes good sense for the ministries and the Folketing to be united, but there are so many governments that could be moved out. And I do not think it necessarily makes any sense that 36 out of 43 governments are here in Copenhagen. There you could probably choose to move some of them out. I would actually like to give an example, which I also included in a brief comment. For a large part of our agricultural knowledge and businesses are located in Jutland. There's the Foulum Research Centre. There are Danish Crown, which has many of its production jobs there. We have Arla. There are plenty of big farms. Why, then, has the Agricultural College been converted to be in the middle of Copenhagen, where there is no shadow of agricultural land? Why is the Danish Food Authority in the middle of Copenhagen? It might be a good idea for us to consider moving it out so that we could have the competences put together, and thus we could perhaps also boost growth, because we could then draw on the skills and develop the professions which, at least in terms of food, are a really big business for us. There are a number of other examples to be called into question, for example. The Danish Environmental Protection Agency. Or why is the Danish Nature Agency in the middle of Copenhagen, where there is no nature again? Perhaps the Danish Energy Agency could be moved to Esbjerg, as several rapporteurs have also mentioned. That is precisely where the jobs lie, and the sector in which they are concerned lies there. Why is Banedanmark at the end of the railroad? Maybe they shouldn't lie in, for example. Fredericia, the hub? The Road Directorate is located at one of the most expensive addresses in the middle of Copenhagen. At least you could save some rent there if you moved it somewhere else. It may be the Board of Cards and Cadastres, and there is the Danish Food Authority, which I have mentioned. In other words, there are a large number of state jobs that could at least be taken into account in a study of which makes sense and then you can make a plan based on it. Because it is clear that everything should not be moved at once. Because it is true that the risk is that it will give a professional dive, and it will certainly in the short term. But as we were also told at the hearing in the Rural Committee, there has therefore not been a professional dive in the Tinglyningen in Hobro or in the SKAT in Ringkøbing. It may have been there at the beginning because you had to recruit some new employees, but now you are fully running and you have some good government jobs and it works very well. I do not think that state jobs should be set up, for example. close to politicians as it is now. Nor should it necessarily be close to the ministries, so that this is the way to deal with the rulers. And it should not necessarily be close to a university because of recruitment. It is therefore easy to recruit, even if it is located in rural areas, and this has also been seen in the good examples, among others. Tingling and scaffolding. Conversely, we can see that there has been constant pressure to centralise things in Copenhagen. There has been a reorganisation of government departments. There has been a merger of the Road Directorate's road centres, and even the Coast Directorate, which was in Lemvig, and now moved to Copenhagen. It is therefore a pity that we have gone in the wrong direction. Then I would like to say that, as I said, there have been a lot of good examples, and that is also some of the points that have been made at the hearing in the Rural Committee. I think that we should just listen to it, but also look to other countries, such as the United States. England, which has had some very good experiences with relocation. I would say that this is one of the things that, of course, makes it important to move out. But it is clear, as the Liberal Alliance says, that this is not what creates much higher growth, because it is, after all, in the private sector. But it is also the case that you now have a potential employee who you would like to recruit for, for example. Vestas in Lem, and the wife is going with it may of course be the husband too. After all, it is easier to get over there if there are also some government jobs that may also employ highly educated people. Then there are some other jobs that can then be offered to enter. So this generally makes it easier to get more skilled labour in rural areas if there are also some government agencies that are located there. Then you can have your spouse with you, so there is a greater chance of moving to the area as well. I would say that then there is also the aspect of that, that of course it helps to pump some money into the community. Could be the hairdressers. The kids need to be looked after. You also have to shop in Netto or Kvickly, or where you now shop. So in this way it also creates some derived effects in the local area, which can help to hold hands during private employment. So I would like to say briefly in conclusion that I can only see that everyone will win at the relocation of state jobs. That is why I think we should be able to be more clear in the saliva of the Danish Parliament, so that we not only say that we would prefer to see that if new state jobs are to be created, they should be moved out, but the existing jobs are not really being dealt with. The previous rapporteur I think it was from the Conservatives, or it was from the Liberal Alliance, I do not remember saying that there will actually be fewer state jobs overall, as is also shown in the statement that needs to be dealt with in a moment. In other words, when there are no new state jobs, nobody will be moved out either, it will therefore be the status quo. I think we should take a little harder action and clearly state together that we actually want to move state jobs to rural areas and to the province. In conclusion, however, I would like to express my thanks for a good debate, a good exchange of views, and then we must hope that it will bear fruit in the future with the relocation of state jobs.

Some might say that these are individual small steps, but I disagree with that. Indeed, action is needed in many areas in order to achieve an overall effect, and that is what the government has done. This is because people who both want and can afford to buy cannot obtain loans from their mortgage or bank because the turnover on the housing market in the specific area is too low. It is an evil spiral, which means that the negative development in the outermost regions becomes self-enhancing. New loan offers have recently been made to the property owners. In addition, we need to look at how we can place public jobs so that they can contribute to the development of the country's outermost regions. ParlaMint-DK_20150506195755	Is the ministerial raffle over? It doesn't say Thomas Danielsen, Minister of Transport, but we'll see about that. Thank you for the floor. In February 2015, a judgment, the so-called NORTRA judgment, on section 18 of the bus services Act was handed down. 2 and Section 5 of the Taxi Act. 5, which means that any collective agreement for drivers, regardless of its content, complies with the relevant provisions of the Bus Driving Act and the Taxi Act. Transport of goods by road § 6 para. 3, is also included in the bill. The draft law seeks to clarify the rules so as to specify the requirements to be met by the agreement. It is proposed that the holder of a licence to carry out limousine services, medical services, public services, taxi services, road haulage and bus services should comply with the provisions on the pay and working conditions of drivers contained in the collective agreements concluded with the most representative social partners in Denmark and applicable throughout the Danish territory. This is an interference in the Danish model, and it is worrying. On the other hand, however, it is necessary to clarify the rules in the wake of the NORTRA judgment. However, I must express my surprise at the fact that the draft law does not report any details of the above-mentioned collective agreements in force throughout Denmark. Unfortunately, the draft law does not reveal which aspects of the labour market are considered representative either. With this bill and the subsequent amendment, the Government has gone a step further than the NORTRA judgment and has used the judgment on the OST permits to propose more regulation in the whole taxi sector. ATAX, the national employers' organisation in the taxi sector, says in their consultation answer that there are some smaller cities in Denmark where they are not represented. It is therefore surprising that the government will give the deathblow to all local taxi agreements on the basis of a ruling on the OST permits. Limousine driving is often characterised by freelance drivers, and the bill will make that type of employment impossible. It is a necessity for the industry to hire drivers freelance because it is a seasonal profession with considerable variations in the amount of driving. The industry will not be able to pay drivers all year round with a fixed hourly wage, such as: Krifa's or 3F's agreement presupposes. The Left therefore proposes that limousine driving be exempted or withdrawn from the legislative proposal. In conclusion, I regret to say that it is bound to be lawless that the consultation responses to such an important legislative proposal have come so late when we only received them on Monday evening. I also note that a number of the consultation parties want an economic consultation in order to clarify the economic consequences of the proposal before it is adopted. To sum up, the Left is in favour of the aim of the legislative proposal to create healthy and clear conditions of competition, but, as I said, we have a number of issues that we need to clarify and discuss in committee before we finally take a position on the legislative proposal as a whole.

After all, this is an intervention in the Danish model. That is something we cannot avoid, and then we will have to see if it can be balanced so that we can support it. We are not very sure of that at first sight. On the other hand, however, we also recognise that there must of course be certain rules on which to rely. The draft law refers to the representative, and I think that is also fair to take a position on that. Who is it, and how is it defined, and is it a satisfying concept? We'll have to look into that. Then there is a request from the taxi area in the consultation answers which points out that in many of the local areas there are already problems, and there will be some places where the agreements can no longer apply, and why should they be abolished in the light of this? They don't understand that, and I don't understand that either. At the same time, I would like to say that we have had those hearings far too late, Monday evening this week. We have not had time to go to the bottom in detail and find out what is up and down in those consultation responses. We have talked about this several times in the Folketing and in the course of the parliamentary work, but it would be useful if we could get them something earlier so that we could deal more thoroughly with them. Then there are those who offer limousine driving, as we know from the industry are paid with bonuses and commission wages, and there are plenty of indications that if you cannot, it simply cannot be done to maintain that profession. Therefore, we may think that it is a good idea to pull it out of the bill. With the statements I have made here, I have tried to be positive to the best of my ability, but I really do think that there are too many things to be negative about, and I therefore say that on the basis that there is now, it does not seem that we can support it, but it may be that we can define and clarify things in committee so that we can finally join in anyway, but that requires a great deal of change.

ParlaMint-DK_20150506202156	Thank you. Thank you to the Conservative rapporteur. Then it's the Minister of Transport.
ParlaMint-DK_20150506202202	Thank you. Thank you for your comments. I would like to start by saying that today I was invited to the Technical Business School Centre in Hvidovre and greeted some of the young men and women who have just started a driver training course. The good news is that last year there were only 30, while this year they are actually 130, and they all have agreements on apprenticeships in companies, so there is real demand for young people who look forward to a good long life and a good education, vocational training to become drivers in all sorts of different types of occupations. The pride I met out there and the expectations they were full of was really great. I have no expectation whatsoever that some of them are watching Parliament's television right now and here. Actually, I don't think the young people are spending their time on that. Nevertheless, we must have their concerns with us. They had, in addition to their pride in the subject they had to enter, and their expectations also had concerns, not least about the very question we are dealing with now and here. Exactly this question was one of the things we were actually talking about, because they were completely up to date on the sentencing that has come and which is why we are here now. I think it is important that it is not just about dry speeches and other things; it is actually about some young people right now who are entering an industry that is so much challenged. If we as politicians are not aware of our responsibility here, then there is no future in that industry, so there is actually more at stake than that. The aim of the bill is to ensure that all drivers sitting behind a steering wheel in a Danish truck, bus, limousine, sick transport car or public service car have proper pay and working conditions. That's basically just what we're working for. It is therefore proposed that the road haulage operator should follow the collective agreements concluded by the most representative social partners in Denmark, which apply throughout Denmark. To those parties that are sceptical, I would just like to say that this has always been the case until the ruling of the Supreme Court of February this year changed the situation, and that is also why it has been urgent for me and for a large part of the parties of the Danish Parliament to correct that situation, so as to go back to what it was like before and how it was practiced before the Supreme Court ruling. The judgment stated that any collective agreement for drivers complied with the requirements of the three laws in the area of bus, freight and taxi, and therefore it is necessary to amend the laws in order to ensure the legal status which existed before the judgment. As you may have noticed, I did not mention taxi driving in my introduction, and it is because it is legally uncertain at this stage whether there is an industry standard for pay and working conditions in the taxi sector. I was also present in this Chamber, of course, for the first reading of the proposal, which was mentioned by the Enhedslisten, B 92, where it was pointed out that there is one, and that is also what the Enhedslisten's rapporteur said today. Let me say straight away: I would very much like to make sure that we are absolutely sure whether or not there is one that meets those standards. That is why I have allowed myself to submit an amendment on that particular subject to the Committee on Transport. If the amendment is adopted, it will mean that if there are collective agreements concluded by the most representative social partners, taxi drivers will have to follow them. If that is not the case and those who have said it are wrong, then it is the case that the hauliers must follow the existing agreements. There are, in concrete terms, two changes that we are discussing today. One is to establish that a road haulage operator must comply with the provisions on the pay and working conditions of drivers contained in collective agreements. These must be agreements which are concluded by the most representative social partners in Denmark and which apply throughout Denmark. The second is to propose strengthening the possibility of monitoring compliance with the pay and working conditions of drivers covered by the taxi law, i.e. taxi services, limousine services, medical transportation and public services. This is done by extending the activities of the Contracting Board to include driving under the Taxi Act. For those bus, freight, limousine, sick or public service road hauliers who, at the time of entry into force of the law, may follow agreements which do not meet these requirements, you cannot know that it may well be, but for the sake of safety there will be a transitional provision. The rules here will only apply when the agreement in question expires or can be terminated or when their authorisation is revoked or expired. The reason for the proposed transitional provision is to avoid expropriative intervention. What are the consequences of the bill? These are healthier and fair conditions of competition, better road safety, while at the same time ensuring that drivers in the industry are able to take care of conditions. Thank you for the positive comments and also thanks to those parties that were sceptical and had some questions. I will do everything I can in committee to secure as broad a majority as possible. We owe it to each other, but we owe it not least to the many people who work in the transport sector, and not least to the young people who are currently entering the industry that really needs a helping hand in this matter.
